810 printable course cards

One representative expedition per playable course, with explanations, reference answers and off-screen extensions. Procedural versions in the school can differ. Use Print to select all cards or a page range.

The nine age bands are flexible starting points. These cards are not a full US school curriculum or a promise of grade equivalence.

Open the school

Age 6–7 · Mathematics · course 1/10 · representative expedition

Sandpit shell rescue

Mission

The destination needs 9 objects in total. Load exactly that many and leave the extras in the supply.

Explain the concept

Adding means combining quantities. Subtracting can mean taking away or finding a difference. Draw objects to understand before calculating.

Reference and reasoning

One-to-one counting produces 9 shells in the bucket.

Captain’s tray: 9

Beyond the screen

Recreate “Sandpit shell rescue” on paper or with safe everyday objects. Explain your choices, then change one condition and predict what will happen.

Reflect

What changed? What stayed the same? Can another solution work? What evidence supports your answer?

Age 6–7 · Mathematics · course 2/10 · representative expedition

Crab snack subtraction

Mission

The destination needs 2 objects in total. Load exactly that many and leave the extras in the supply.

Explain the concept

9 − 4 = 5. The shells on the shore show the quantity left.

Reference and reasoning

13 − 2 = 11. The shore shows what remains.

Captain’s tray: 2

Beyond the screen

Reflect

Mission

Explain the concept

A shape is identified by its properties, not just its size or colour.

Reference and reasoning

A shape is identified by its properties, not just its size or colour.

Hexagon → 6 straight sides
Circle → One curved boundary
Square → 4 equal sides and 4 right angles

Mission

Age 6–7 · Mathematics · course 7/10 · representative expedition

Mathematics: Different bucket deliveries · Age 6–7

Mission

The nursery needs 1. Research needs 4. The library ferry needs 7. Do not assume equal loads.

Explain the concept

Represent quantities in more than one way. Check totals, differences, relationships and units instead of assuming every container needs the same amount.

Reference and reasoning

The loads differ: 4 + 1 + 7 = 12 objects delivered.

Research boat: 4
Coral nursery: 1
Library ferry: 7

Beyond the screen

Age 6–7 · Mathematics · course 9/10 · representative expedition

Mathematics: Find the missing shells · Age 6–7

Mission

The total catch is 8. The first basket needs 4, the second 1, and the third gets the rest. Some shells are already loaded: finish the job.

Explain the concept

Represent quantities in more than one way. Check totals, differences, relationships and units instead of assuming every container needs the same amount.

Reference and reasoning

The third basket needs 8 − 4 − 1 = 3. Count existing contents as well as new moves.

Bucket 1: 4
Bucket 2: 1
Bucket 3: 3

Mathematics: Picture-graph harbour · Age 6–7

Mission

Build the data display: Blue boat 4; Green boat 1; Gold boat 7. Each block represents 1 observations.

Explain the concept

Represent quantities in more than one way. Check totals, differences, relationships and units instead of assuming every container needs the same amount.

Reference and reasoning

A bar’s height must match its value and the stated scale. One block means 1, not always one.

Blue boat: 4 blocks, representing 4
Green boat: 1 blocks, representing 1
Gold boat: 7 blocks, representing 7

Age 6–7 · French · world language · course 1/10 · representative expedition

French sound fishing

Mission

Catch matching French word beginnings.

Explain the concept

A syllable is a spoken beat in a word. Clap gently to hear each beat. In this subject, we explore French words; their spelling does not always show how many sounds you hear.

Reference and reasoning

Listen to the first syllable: ma-man, mo-to, ba-teau.

maman → ma
moto → mo
bateau → ba

Beyond the screen

Recreate “French sound fishing” on paper or with safe everyday objects. Explain your choices, then change one condition and predict what will happen.

Reflect

What changed? What stayed the same? Can another solution work? What evidence supports your answer?

French: Read a tiny French log · Age 6–7

Mission

Robin arrive au port avec 4 coquillages. La pluie commence. Robin ouvre un parapluie.

Read the short log, then collect only the claims directly supported by it. Do not invent a person’s age or contradict the weather.

Explain the concept

Use the French text, its grammatical relationships and its clues. Distinguish what is written from what is inferred.

Reference and reasoning

The quantity and action are stated. The age is not given, and the text states that rain begins.

Robin a 4 coquillages.
Robin ouvre un parapluie.

Age 6–7 · Science · course 1/10 · representative expedition

Living garden rescue

Mission

Place each discovery in its home.

Explain the concept

Living things grow and develop, exchange with their environment and belong to lineages that reproduce. Movement alone does not make something alive.

Reference and reasoning

Plants and animals are living organisms. Movement alone does not make an object alive.

Living organisms: 🌱 Bean plant, 🦋 Butterfly, 🌳 Tree
Non-living objects: 🤖 Robot, 🚲 Bicycle, 🪨 Stone

Beyond the screen

Recreate “Living garden rescue” on paper or with safe everyday objects. Explain your choices, then change one condition and predict what will happen.

Science: Compare a fair test · Age 6–7

Mission

Design a comparison to investigate light exposure. Sort what should change and what should stay comparable.

Explain the concept

Connect a testable question, a model, observations and a qualified explanation. Change one condition deliberately and compare evidence.

Reference and reasoning

A controlled comparison varies the factor being investigated while limiting other relevant differences.

Change deliberately: light exposure
Keep comparable: The same type of plant, The same observation period

Beyond the screen

Age 6–7 · History & geography · course 9/10 · representative expedition

History & geography: Before-and-after evidence · Age 6–7

Mission

Arrange the stated fictional case in time. Then explain why time order alone does not prove every causal claim.

Explain the concept

Use location, chronology, context and evidence. Distinguish a source’s message from what it establishes about the world.

Reference and reasoning

Chronology is necessary for many explanations but does not by itself establish all causes.

A fictional storm damages a bridge. → The town documents the damage. → Residents compare repair proposals. → A suitable plan is tested and reviewed.

Beyond the screen

Rebuild “History & geography: Before-and-after evidence · Age 6–7” with drawings, safe objects, spoken explanation or a paper trace. Change one condition and predict the result before testing it.

Reflect

What changed? What stayed the same? Can another solution work? What evidence supports your answer?

Age 6–7 · History & geography · course 10/10 · representative expedition

History & geography: Choose a useful source · Age 6–7

Mission

Inspect what a historical poster can directly show. Separate it from claims that require other evidence.

Explain the concept

Use location, chronology, context and evidence. Distinguish a source’s message from what it establishes about the world.

Reference and reasoning

A source’s existence and message do not automatically prove acceptance or truth.

Shown by the source itself: The date printed on this poster, The words printed on this poster
Needs additional evidence: Everyone believed this poster., The poster’s message was true.

Beyond the screen

Rebuild “History & geography: Choose a useful source · Age 6–7” with drawings, safe objects, spoken explanation or a paper trace. Change one condition and predict the result before testing it.

Reflect

Reference and reasoning

History & geography: Sequence causes and events · Age 8–9

Mission

Arrange the stated fictional case in time. Then explain why time order alone does not prove every causal claim.

Explain the concept

Use location, chronology, context and evidence. Distinguish a source’s message from what it establishes about the world.

Reference and reasoning

Chronology is necessary for many explanations but does not by itself establish all causes.

A fictional storm damages a bridge. → The town documents the damage. → Residents compare repair proposals. → A suitable plan is tested and reviewed.

History & geography: Compare two sources · Age 8–9

Mission

Inspect what a historical poster can directly show. Separate it from claims that require other evidence.

Reference and reasoning

A source’s existence and message do not automatically prove acceptance or truth.

Shown by the source itself: The date printed on this poster, The words printed on this poster
Needs additional evidence: Everyone believed this poster., The poster’s message was true.

Age 9–10 · English & literacy · course 1/10 · representative expedition

There-is bridge

Mission

Match the sentence beginning to a grammatically fitting ending.

Explain the concept

Use there is to introduce one thing. Use there are for more than one thing. “There is a book” introduces a single book.

Reference and reasoning

Use is with a singular thing and are with plural things.

There is → a tree.
There are → three shells.
There is not → a boat here.

The Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen was adopted in 1789 during the French Revolution. It proclaimed principles, but their application was limited and contested: women did not then obtain equal political rights.

Reference and reasoning

An ordered sequence is the foundation for locating developments in time.

1789 → 1848 → 1870 → 1944

Beyond the screen

Recreate “Rights-and-republic timeline” on paper or with safe everyday objects. Explain your choices, then change one condition and predict what will happen.

Age 11–12 · Science · course 1/10 · representative expedition

Evidence laboratory

Mission

Sort entries in a rolling-ball investigation.

Explain the concept

A hypothesis is a possible explanation that can be tested. Design a method, measure and compare the results with your prediction. Unexpected results help you revise an idea; do not change measurements to make yourself right.

Reference and reasoning

Hypotheses predict; observations record; conclusions interpret evidence within limits.

Hypothesis: A smoother surface may let the ball travel farther.
Observation: The measured distances were 40, 42 and 41 cm.
Conclusion: In these trials, this surface gave longer runs.

Beyond the screen

Age 12–13 · French · world language · course 1/10 · representative expedition

Metaphor reef

Mission

Sort these French images by how they connect ideas.

Explain the concept

In French, a comparison explicitly links things with a word such as comme, meaning like or as. A metaphor makes a connection without that explicit comparison word. Its effect depends on context.

Reference and reasoning

Comme and tel explicitly mark comparison; these metaphors do not use such a marker.

Explicit comparison: La mer est comme un miroir., Il court tel un éclair.
Metaphor: La mer est un miroir., Un océan de tristesse.

Reference and reasoning

The conducting loop must be continuous for current to pass through this series model.

Battery + → Closed switch → Lamp → Battery −

Intentional asymmetry · Age 12–13

Mission

Restore the right half of the coral mosaic as a reflection of the left. Choose a colour, then paint.

Explain the concept

Observe how choices of colour, position, pattern and contrast affect an image. Explain your choices.

Reference and reasoning

Reflection reverses left and right across the centre line while keeping corresponding colours.

1 1 3 2 2 3 1 1
3 2 0 2 2 0 2 3
3 0 3 0 0 3 0 3
1 0 1 1 1 1 0 1
2 2 2 0 0 2 2 2

Age 13–14 · Mathematics · course 1/10 · representative expedition

Balance the mystery crate

Mission

Balance 5x + 1 = 26.

Explain the concept

An equation states that two expressions are equal. To preserve equality, perform the same operation on both sides. To solve a × x + b = c, subtract b and then divide by a, provided a is not zero.

Reference and reasoning

Subtract 1 then divide by 5: x = 5.

Crate x: 5 units

Lou ouvre la porte. → La porte est ouverte par Lou.
Le vent pousse le bateau. → Le bateau est poussé par le vent.

Unnecessary private information: Home address, Account password

Age 13–14 · Life & critical thinking · course 10/10 · representative expedition

Life & critical thinking: Measure a community trial · Age 13–14

Mission

Match a stated barrier or uncertainty with a relevant helpful response.

Explain the concept

Consider needs, constraints, privacy and other people’s perspectives. Test a small action and review what actually helps.

Reference and reasoning

A helpful response should address the actual barrier and respect the person’s choices.

The user’s need is uncertain → Ask respectfully and test a small version
Text is too small → Offer larger text
Someone needs quiet → Offer a quieter option without demanding private explanations
Only one input method exists → Offer an alternative input method
Instructions are confusing → Use clear numbered steps

Age 14–15 · Mathematics · course 1/10 · representative expedition

Lighthouse line navigator

Mission

Set f(x) = ax + b so that f(0) = -2 and f(3) = 1.

Explain the concept

A linear function with an intercept can be written f(x) = ax + b. The coefficient a is the change in f when x increases by 1; b is f(0). Keep units consistent. A signed slope can be negative.

Reference and reasoning

b = -2. The change over three x-units is 3, giving a = 1.

Slope a: 1 
Intercept b: -2

Beyond the screen

Recreate “Lighthouse line navigator” on paper or with safe everyday objects. Explain your choices, then change one condition and predict what will happen.

Represent quantities in more than one way. Check totals, differences, relationships and units instead of assuming every container needs the same amount.

Reference and reasoning

Undo the stated relationships while preserving units and equality. The requested quantity is 4.

Unknown quantity: 4

Long-term climate statistics: Thirty-year rainfall average, Temperature trend across several decades

Mission

Explain the concept

Consider needs, constraints, privacy and other people’s perspectives. Test a small action and review what actually helps.

Reference and reasoning

A feasible plan can meet several different needs while respecting a total constraint. There may be more than one reasonable solution.

Practice time: at least 4

Helping time: at least 4
Budget 21; reserve at least 2; multiple solutions accepted.

Life & critical thinking: Evaluate a project outcome · Age 14–15

Mission

Match a stated barrier or uncertainty with a relevant helpful response.

Explain the concept

Consider needs, constraints, privacy and other people’s perspectives. Test a small action and review what actually helps.

Reference and reasoning

A helpful response should address the actual barrier and respect the person’s choices.

The user’s need is uncertain → Ask respectfully and test a small version
Text is too small → Offer larger text
Someone needs quiet → Offer a quieter option without demanding private explanations
Only one input method exists → Offer an alternative input method
Instructions are confusing → Use clear numbered steps

Reflection and identity · Age 14–15

Observe how choices of colour, position, pattern and contrast affect an image. Explain your choices.

Reference and reasoning

Mission

Use listening and a visual model to compare pulse, rhythm, pitch, structure and expression.

Reference and reasoning

A relative instruction specifies a change from the preceding key, not a new absolute key number. Listen and check each transition.

3 → 5 → 3 → 4 → 5 → 4 → 3 → 1 → 1

Phrase 2: 3 blocks, representing 3
Phrase 3: 4 blocks, representing 4
